❯ I kind of want to understand how our generation logic works as well for the   
  interview formats you mentioned were scoped. Ie refactoring, debugging,       
  lld... are these templated or is it really just generated freely by the model 
  based on the spec?                                                            
                                                                                
  I want to get to the point where its the latter. I think our goal should be   
  to provide the right harness for the model to generate mocks to whatever      
  rounds currently exist right now, since interviews are getting so             
  personalized and varied.                                                      

⏺ Let me ground this in the actual generation path before answering — checking
  how generateInto runs the generator and what's format-specific in code.

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 2 files (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ It's already the latter — and it's worth being precise about where the freedom
  lives and where it deliberately doesn't, because the line is the product's
  core design decision ("capabilities, not categories" + "fixed ruler, generated
  content," CEO review 2026-07-31).

  There are no templates

  There is no question bank, no per-format generator, no "debugging template."
  There is one prompt for every format (prompts/generate-round.md), and
  generation is an agentic call (generate.ts): a headless claude -p is dropped
  into an empty directory with file-write and shell access, up to 80 turns, and
  it writes an original codebase from scratch — 4-8 production-style source
  files, a behavioral test suite, the planted bug or scaffold or diff, the
  problem statement, the title, and the six per-dimension judge expectations. It
  runs the tests itself, fixes what's broken, self-verifies, and only then
  writes the manifest. Domain, entities, storyline — all free, steered entirely
  by the ROUND_BRIEF: your description, your pasted reference material, the
  planned title, and the gap-graph emphasis note.

  This is why "LLD," "refactoring," "debugging" don't appear anywhere in the
  generation code. They aren't code paths. An LLD round is just blank + 
  all_failing with a brief that says class design; refactoring is all_passing;
  debugging is one_failing_test. When you did the Palantir prep, the generator
  produced original LLD-flavored problems from Erik's notes and your reference
  question — nothing retrieved, nothing templated.

  What IS fixed — and why you want it fixed

  The harness is three closed things, and each is closed for a reason:

  1. Proof obligations (CHECK_REQUIREMENT_BLOCKS, 4 kinds). These look like
  templates but aren't — they're what the validator can mechanically prove.
  "Exactly one test fails, and fixing the bug makes all pass." "Every test fails
  on the untouched scaffold." The generator is told which proof applies and
  never writes its own passing criteria. This is the thing that makes free
  generation trustworthy: validateProblem re-proves the claim after the agent
  finishes, and a problem that fails proof never reaches you.
  2. The capability vocabulary (5 axes) — what the session runner can physically
  do: voice on/off, test runner present, countdown, starting artifact,
  grade-once.
  3. The six judge dimensions — the fixed ruler, so fifteen sessions accumulate
  into one profile instead of fragmenting.

  So your stated goal — "provide the right harness for the model to generate 
  mocks to whatever rounds currently exist" — is not a destination, it's the
  shipped architecture. The header of round-spec.ts says it outright: "the
  answer to 'do you support format X' is a property of this vocabulary, not a
  roadmap item."

  The honest distance that remains

  Where the harness still bounds "whatever rounds exist," in rough order of
  realness:

  - Proof kinds are the true bottleneck. A genuinely new round shape is new only
  if it needs a new provable criterion. Example: an optimization round ("make
  this pass under 200ms") — the brief can flavor it, but no check kind can prove
  it; all_passing would accept a non-optimizing solution. Teaching
  checkManifest a perf_threshold proof is what "supporting" that round means.
  This is the designed extension point.
  - Runtimes: TS and Python only. A Java/Go/SQL round gets coerced into the
  nearest TS/Py equivalent.
  - The realism ceiling: the debugging proof block currently mandates "pure
  logic + in-memory state, no HTTP server, no database" — gauntlet-proven for
  reliability, but it means "debug this API service" rounds get simplified.
  Relaxing that is a generation-quality experiment, not an architecture change.
  - Interviewer personalization is the thinnest layer: the problem is fully
  personalized, but the interviewer's probing style is generic — a
  Palantir-style interviewer versus an Amazon-style one isn't expressed
  anywhere. If interviews are getting personalized and varied, this is arguably
  the next surface where "harness, not templates" should apply: a generated
  per-round interviewer brief, gated the same way expectations are.
  - Single-problem sessions — a 3-section OA is three queue items, not one
  session with stages.

  If you want to push toward the vision, the leverage order I'd argue for:
  interviewer briefs and the realism ceiling are cheap experiments inside the
  existing harness; new proof kinds and runtimes are real extensions you add
  when a target demands them — exactly how the vocabulary was designed to grow.

❯ ok - so going back to the ceo plan we just revised - what would be the        
  changes we would make here give me a summary                                  

⏺ Here's the revised plan's build scope in one pass — what actually changes if
  we implement it:

  Data model (queue.ts)

  QueueItem gains a discriminated kind: 'external' variant (absent = generated,
  so every existing queue on disk stays valid). Its payload is exactly one of:
  - problems: [{title, url?}] — named LC problems, link per-title
  - fallback: {count, topic} — "3 graph mediums" when no titles attest
  - task: string (+ optional url/note) — generic rows: "mock with Erik", "read
  DDIA ch. 5"

  plus a single-slot debrief (overwritten on re-done — that's the fix for the
  evidence-duplication bug codex caught). Generic task rows carry no spec_id; LC
  rows keep their spec so memory tags and pace still count the round.

  The router (clarify + prompt)

  The one rule at the heart of it: autograded pure-LC round → external items; 
  anything an interviewer touches, or any non-LC shape → native generation
  (pasted LC problems still produce same-species originals — never verbatim).
  Candidate-named outside activities bypass the router entirely and schedule
  verbatim as task rows. Your pasted lists (Blind 75, a friend's sheet) are
  tier-1 evidence and schedule word-for-word; model-originated titles ship only
  self-attested, falling back to topic rows at zero attestation — your T1 call,
  with codex's dissent and the allowlist hardening path recorded (#21).

  Gates (gateExternal, new pure module)

  Exclusivity (exactly one payload shape), the leetcode.com URL pattern gate (a
  failing url is dropped, title kept — also what makes a model string safe as an
  href), cap of 6 problems per row with overflow splitting into additional
  days.

  Adapt (adapt.ts)

  The critical F1 fix: external items are invisible to spec round-robin (a
  status-based selector would otherwise re-point your LC rows onto specs and
  destroy them — same bug class as the resurrection fix). E2's edits travel in
  their own external_changes[] diff lane with full record/recovery parity,
  previewed and approved like everything else.

  Server + UI

  POST /api/external-done: idempotent done/un-done on the existing
  status/done_at fields; un-done keeps the debrief; "remove" means skipped,
  never deletion. Timeline rows are kind-aware — titles, open ↗, copy-titles,
  done checkbox, inline debrief after checking — and never show Generate/Start.
  Pace counts external items; undone ones age like pending, never red.

  Explicitly not in this plan

  Gap-graph ingestion of debriefs (deferred to drills, #13 — T2), the slug
  allowlist (#21), drills themselves, native DS&A ambitions, verbatim LC import,
  new canvases.

  Size: 8 tasks, ~CC 2-3 hrs total (tasks JSONL written for /autoplan).

  One clarification given the generation discussion we just had: this plan is
  entirely scheduling-layer — it doesn't touch the generator or its harness. The
  ideas from that conversation (per-round interviewer briefs, a perf-proof
  check kind, relaxing the no-HTTP realism ceiling) would be separate scope on
  the generation side, unscoped and unreviewed so far.

✻ Crunched for 36s

❯ I know that we mentioned to let LLMs natively print out leetcode titles. I    
  guess on external tasks, is there a component of research involved to         
  actually pull the external? If not, would it be worth incorporating?          

⏺ No — there's deliberately zero retrieval in the external-item path, and that's
  load-bearing, not an omission. Titles come from exactly two sources: your 
  pasted material, scheduled verbatim (a NeetCode list, a friend's sheet —
  tier-1 evidence), or model priors with self-attestation (your T1 call). The
  plan even states it structurally: "no network check — the product has no fetch
  path by design."

  Would adding research be worth it? It splits into two different questions with
  two different answers:

  Verification retrieval — "does leetcode.com/problems/two-sum actually exist?"
  This is the legitimate version: mechanical, no judgment, just fact-checking a
  slug. But a live HTTP check buys you almost nothing over the static allowlist 
  we already logged as TODO #21: ~500 famous slugs bundled as a file gets you a
  genuinely mechanical gate with zero network, zero rate-limiting risk, zero new
  failure surface. If invented titles ever show up in real plans, #21 is the
  answer — not fetch. The one thing live checks add over a static list is
  coverage of the long tail, and the long tail is precisely where you want the
  topic-row fallback anyway.

  Discovery retrieval — "which problems does Amazon's OA actually use?" This is
  the version I'd argue hard against, because it's the deleted research feature
  reincarnated in a narrower costume. The good data (LeetCode's company tags) is
  Premium-login-walled — the same class of problem that killed the Glassdoor
  idea. The public data (GitHub gists, "Amazon OA 2025" blog posts) is stale
  secondhand reporting — exactly the tier-3 evidence whose confident wrongness
  produced the 40% rejection rate and the Palantir miss. And the failure mode is
  identical: the plan says "Amazon asks these 6 problems" with product
  authority, the candidate trusts it over their own prep sheet, and we've aimed
  them wrong again — just at problem granularity instead of round granularity.

  There's also an honest asymmetry worth naming, which is why T1 was a
  defensible call in a way the old research never was: model priors on famous LC
  titles are far stronger than model priors on company round shapes were. Two
  Sum's existence is about as in-distribution as facts get; "Palantir's 2026 OA
  format" never was. So the residual risk you accepted is small, bounded by the
  fallback shape, and has a mechanical hardening path waiting in #21.

  The strongest "research" available here stays what it's been all day: the
  candidate pastes the list they trust, and it schedules word-for-word. If you
  want anything more, I'd promote #21 from P3 into the bridge build (~30 min CC)
  rather than open a fetch path — say the word and I'll fold it into the task
  list.

※ recap: Zenkai scoping: we shipped the research-cut plus plan-adaptation build, then locked the external-practice bridge plan (LC rows via 8 tasks, CEO-reviewed). You chose to stop before building; next action is /plan-eng-review, then implement the bridge. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ So when i was working in cowork, im pretty sure cowork did a search of        
  leetcode questions or amazon stuff before making the plan. Is it possible to  
  just mimic how cowork does it? It doesn't need to be all or nothing for how   
  to index on search results, but i think at least some research would be       
  helpful in choosing leetcode questions for example                            

⏺ You're right, and this is a real distinction I flattened. Let me update
  honestly rather than defend the fence.

  The research feature that died was answering "what shape is your round?" —
  high stakes, and it kept colliding with things you already knew (the
  HackerRank preview, Erik's notes). Wrong answer = you prep the wrong format
  for weeks. Problem-selection research answers "which LC problems should fill 
  this row?" — and the failure math is completely different:

  1. The stakes are low and asymmetric. If search surfaces problems that are
  "commonly Amazon" and Amazon asks different ones, you still did real reps on
  real problems. Practicing a slightly-off-target valid problem is cheap and
  transfers — that's literally the "include, never adjudicate" asymmetry
  argument, working for search this time.
  2. There's no firsthand evidence to collide with. The old feature's sin was
  overriding tier-1 knowledge. But which specific problems to grind is a gap you
  genuinely don't have better information about — and the evidence hierarchy
  always said research legitimately "fills gaps the candidate left open."
  3. Your Cowork experience is itself tier-1 evidence about what works, and I
  was arguing base rates against it. That's my own rule violated.

  And there's a bonus I undersold: search partially solves the T1 hallucination 
  problem better than self-attestation does. A search result containing
  leetcode.com/problems/course-schedule-ii is a retrieved slug, not an invented
  one — the URL pattern gate we already designed can mechanically verify shape,
  and retrieved slugs are overwhelmingly real. Codex's objection to
  model-invented titles and your desire for search actually converge here.

  The fenced design, so this doesn't recreate what we buried:

  SEARCH IS ALLOWED TO ANSWER:  which problems fill an already-confirmed
                                external LC row (leaf content)
  SEARCH NEVER TOUCHES:         round shape, specs, native generation
  WHEN:                         at plan build / adapt, AFTER you confirm the
                                round is external — never on the intake path
  GATE:                         retrieved titles+slugs pass the existing URL
                                pattern gate; cap 6; degrades quietly to
                                priors+attestation, then to topic rows

  Mechanically it's a narrow, deliberate revival of the deleted researcher —
  claude -p with WebSearch, one call per external round — and the hardened
  parsing lessons (normalizeCitation, balanced-brace) are sitting in git history
  at 6d57131 waiting to be reverted selectively. Cost is ~30-60s added to
  "Building your plan…" only when an external round exists.

  This amends the reviewed plan (T1's mechanism changes from priors-first to
  search-first), so it's your call:

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · D3 — Amend the bridge: search-sourced LC problem selection?

     Project/branch: interview_prep on main; amends the external-practice-bridge
     CEO plan (revises decision T1's mechanism).

     ELI10: When the plan builds an LC row, a fenced search agent looks up which
     problems are commonly associated with that company's OA and fills the row 
     with retrieved titles+links — the thing Cowork did for you. Search stays 
     banned from round-shape questions; it only picks leaf content for rows you 
     already confirmed as external, and every result passes the mechanical URL 
     gate.

     Stakes if we pick wrong: without it, rows carry model-memory titles or 
     generic topics — workable but less targeted than what Cowork gave you; with
     it fenced badly, we recreate the confident-wrong-specificity failure — the
     fence (leaf content only, post-confirmation, quiet degrade) is the whole 
     design.

     Recommendation: A — low-stakes gap-filling is what research was always 
     legitimately for, your Cowork experience is direct evidence it helps, and 
     retrieved slugs beat self-attested ones on the exact risk codex flagged. → 
     Add fenced search to the bridge (Recommended)

⏺ The founding design doc closes the argument. It names exactly two value

  0A. Premise challenge — your instinct is correct, and it's over-determined

  Four independent lines of evidence, all pointing the same way:

  1. The design doc (2026-07-27) names the two failures this product exists to 
  fix: "Format: nobody preps the round companies actually give. Memory: nothing 
  carries what you learned about yourself from session one into session seven."
  Format is the generator + RoundSpec + validator. Memory is the judge + gap
  graph. Planning is not on the list. It grew as scaffolding around the two
  things that are.

  2. The only real demand signal you have was for memory, verbatim: "with the 
  memory layer, I would pay." Nobody has ever said they'd pay for a timeline.

  3. The commit log, quantified. Of your last 25 commits, 21 touched the 
  planning layer; 4 touched the mock engine. Everything after 2326bcf (targets +
  intake) has been planning, UI, or identity. The mock engine — RoundSpec,
  validator dispatch, session runner, judge, interviewer, voice — was
  essentially finished in July and has barely been touched since.

    LOC          PLANNING            MOCK ENGINE
    server       1,294 (queue,       3,110 (session, judge, interviewer,
                 clarify, adapt,            generate, validate, gap-graph,
                 plan-topics, intake)       voice, chrome)
    client         720 (app.js)        511 (session, voice, presence)
    tests           56                  118
    ---------------------------------------------------------------
    recent build   21 of last 25 commits    4 of last 25

  The codebase is still 2:1 mock engine. Your attention has been 5:1 planning.

  4. The landscape says nobody wins on planning. interviewing.io differentiates
  on human interviewer supply ($225/session, late-stage calibration).
  Exponent/Pramp differentiates on curriculum and peer matching ($79/mo). The
  category has split into live-assist tools and rehearsal tools — and not one of
  the surveyed products has an executable canvas: a real repo, a real test
  runner, mechanically-proven problems, a judge reading a trace of what you
  actually did. Google retired Interview Warmup in 2026. Planning is table
  stakes; nobody's moat.

  Layer 3, first principles — and this one reframes the whole question. You've
  been treating Cowork as a competitor that makes your planning redundant. It's
  the opposite: a general agent is the best planner any vertical product will 
  ever have access to, because it has the entire conversation with the user. You
  cannot out-plan it, and trying is a war you lose slowly. But a vertical
  product holding an execution surface the agent cannot reach — a Docker IDE, a
  live voice interviewer, a validator that proves exactly one test fails — gets
  stronger as that agent improves, because the agent becomes a better planner
  pointed at your product. The general agent isn't the competitor. It's the
  distribution channel.

  0B. What already exists

  /api/accept-spec already accepts specs and builds a queue — an import endpoint
  is its sibling, not new architecture. RoundSpec is already the config
  contract between planning and execution. Today a Cowork plan can be pasted
  into intake as context, but clarify collapses it into 1-2 specs and
  proposeQueue + plan-topics re-derive the schedule and re-name every round — so
  Cowork's specific item list, ordering, and titles get thrown away. That
  fidelity loss is precisely what an import path fixes, and it makes
  plan-topics.ts (121 LOC of LLM naming) redundant on that path.

  0C. Dream state

    CURRENT                        THIS DECISION              12-MONTH IDEAL
    Zenkai owns the whole      →   freeze planning       →    Zenkai is THE
  execution
    chain: intake→clarify→         features; add an           engine for
  technical
    queue→timeline→adapt           import contract;           practice. Plans
  arrive
    (~2,000 LOC) plus the          redirect capacity          from anywhere —
  its own
    mock engine                    to format + memory         thin intake,
  Cowork, a
                                                              coach. The moat is
  canvas

⏺ That reframes the whole review, and it means my recommendation was wrong. Let
  me say that plainly before anything else.

  I attacked a claim you never made. I spent the analysis arguing planning isn't
  differentiated — you already knew that. You built it so users don't
  context-switch. And under that goal, my Approach A is the worst option on the
  board: an import path requires leaving Zenkai, going to Cowork, coming back,
  and pasting. It manufactures the exact friction the feature exists to remove.
  Same for MCP, which only reduces friction if Cowork is your home rather than
  Zenkai.

  Let me also retract the T9 kill. I argued the LC searcher "rebuilds a Cowork
  capability." Under the friction lens that's backwards: without it, finding LC
  problems means leaving the product. That's containment, not novelty. T9 was 
  right and I was wrong to cut it.

  Now let me give your intuition a stronger form than "switching is annoying,"
  because I think there's an architectural argument hiding inside it.

  Why native planning is actually correct — the version worth defending

  The plan and the practice have to live together because they're one loop: plan
  → practice → judge → gap → re-plan. Cowork can do step 1. It structurally
  cannot do 2, 3, or 4 — it never sees your session, your trace, or your
  verdicts. And because it can't see those, it can't do step 5 well either. A
  Cowork plan is frozen at what you told it. A Zenkai plan can re-aim on
  evidence about how you actually performed.

  So the honest split is sharper than "planning is commodity":

  - Planning as authoring (intake, questions, scheduling, LC rows) — genuinely
  commodity. Cowork does it well. Build it to parity, exactly as you said,
  purely so nobody leaves.
  - Planning as feedback (the plan changing because of what the judge saw) —
  impossible anywhere else. That's not scaffolding around the moat; it is the
  moat, extended one step.

  Which surfaces the actual finding of this review:

  The loop is open at the one joint that matters

  Let me confirm the wiring before I claim it.

  I deliberately fixed nothing — several of these are design calls I shouldn't
  make for you, and two are one-liners you'll want to see first.

  Your six questions, answered

  1. Generation progress is static. Worse than you thought — the bar is
  animation: slide 1.5s infinite, byte-identical at second 5 and minute 8. And
  the estimate is wrong: I timed a real generation at 422 seconds (7 minutes),
  not "about 5 minutes." Real signal is available for free: the agent writes
  files as it goes (problem.json, tests/, source), so file count or the
  .validated marker gives you honest phases instead of a decorative loop.

  2. Sessions can't be killed — and it's worse than "annoying." /api/end grades
  the session but never tears down the container or the :3200 server. So
  session_live stays true forever, which hides the Start button on every plan
  and makes /api/launch 409. There is no kill path anywhere in the UI (app.js
  has zero kill/end/stop references). Every completed round soft-locks the 
  product until you open a terminal. I found one orphaned from 33 minutes before
  I started, from a different target, already graded.

  3. Start latency. The session actually boots in 2 seconds — but the banner
  promises "takes a minute to boot," and it renders 482 pixels above the button 
  you just clicked (measured). The button itself stays enabled with an unchanged
  label. So you click, nothing changes where you're looking, and the one piece
  of feedback is off-screen. Your Submit button already does this right: it
  becomes "Grading…" and disables instantly. Same treatment on Start closes it.

  4. Separate session server. For local single-user it's right, and it proved
  itself during this QA — I accidentally killed the app mid-generation and the
  detached generator kept running while disk-derived state recovered it. For
  production it's a hard blocker: const CONTAINER = 'ip-session' plus hardcoded
  ports 3200/3100 make it globally single-tenant. Two users would fight over one
  container name, and session start does docker rm -f ip-session — which would
  kill the other person's interview. Production needs per-session names, dynamic
  ports, a routing proxy, and a lifecycle manager. Not a rewrite; the same
  architecture with identity added.

  5. One task per day / not comprehensive. Two real causes. Pace is 3
  rounds/week over 18 days, so 6 items land ~3 days apart and every gap renders
  as a dated blank row (11 of 15 visible rows empty on Palantir). And separately
  — only the TODAY item is actionable at all. Future rows are inert text with
  no Generate button, so you can't build tomorrow's problem tonight. The dead
  days are exactly the hole TODOS #13 (drills) was deferred for.

  6. Completed sessions not reflected — confirmed, and it's a renderer bug, not 
  a data bug. bucketIntoDays builds the past band from back = 4 down to back = 
  1: yesterday through four days ago, never today. The TODAY row shows only
  non-done items. So anything finished today is invisible everywhere. I
  completed your Amazon season and got: progress bar 100%, "3 / 3 done", and an
  entirely empty timeline reading "nothing scheduled — the plan resumes 
  tomorrow." The data was perfect — status, session_id, done_at all correct. The
  plan just hides your best day.

  Three you didn't ask about

  - The orphan sweep lies during healthy generations. It marks
  detached-but-running generations failed on app restart. Self-heals when
  .validated lands, but the retry button is live in that window and would spawn
  a second claude -p agent into the same directory — double spend, corrupted
  output.
  - No way back from a graded session. The session page has zero links. You're
  stranded on :3200.
  - The editor opens on the previous round's files. Launching the Amazon DP
  round showed tabs for ledger.ts, shipments.ts, stockLedger.ts and an active
  tab of warehouse/floorplan.py — a file not even in this round's tree.
  .ide-data persists openvscode's workspace across sessions.

  Also worth knowing: lsof -ti:3200 | xargs kill kills your app server too (it
  polls that port as a client). Safe form is lsof -nP -iTCP:3200 -sTCP:LISTEN 
  -t. I learned that by killing your app mid-QA.

  What I'd fix first

  ISSUE-001 (teardown + a kill affordance) and ISSUE-002 (show today's
  completions) are the two that matter — together they're why the loop feels
  broken at exactly the moments it should feel good. Both are small. Want me to
  fix those two, or the whole Critical+High set? The parked CEO review on
  planning-vs-mocks is also still open whenever you want to return to it.

✻ Brewed for 21m 22s

     edit
     Fix the QA Findings: Session Lifecycle, Timeline Truth, Generation Feedback

     Context

     End-to-end UX QA (report:
     .gstack/qa-reports/qa-report-zenkai-2026-08-02.md) found 13
     issues. The two critical ones compound into "the loop feels broken at the
     moments it
     should feel best": every finished session leaves a live container that
     soft-locks all
     launches with no in-app escape (ISSUE-001), and rounds completed today are
     invisible on
     the timeline, so finishing a season renders as emptiness (ISSUE-002). This
     plan fixes
     all 13.

     User decisions (D1–D4): the in-app kill discards, never grades (trace kept
     for CLI
     rejudge); done-today renders as ✓ rows pinned above TODAY plus
     done-for-today /
     season-complete states; Generate appears on any pending row (one-at-a-time
     still
     server-enforced); runs of 2+ empty future days collapse into one thin quiet
     row.

     Key facts from exploration: the session server never exits and never tears
     down the
     container (session.ts — teardown exists only on SIGINT/SIGTERM at :780,
     which nothing
     sends because the app spawns it detached). sessionLive() (app.ts:49) counts
     any 200
     from /api/status as live, and /api/status (session.ts:549) never reports
     ended.
     Workspace tab state lives in browser IndexedDB keyed by folder URI
     (constant
     /home/workspace/problem → state leaks across problems); .ide-data settings
     never
     reach the workbench UI (KNOWN ISSUE comment, session.ts:139 — named fix:
     injection
     through our proxy). Generation records no start time anywhere; file order
     during a run
     is source → tests → docs → problem.json → .validated.

     ---
     A. Session lifecycle (ISSUE-001, 004, 012, 013 — Critical)

     server/src/session.ts:
     - Extract teardownContainer() (= docker rm -f ip-session) from the signal
     handler.
     - /api/end handler: after finalize() returns and the response is flushed,
     call
     teardownContainer(). (One-shot docker exec grading happens inside finalize,
     before teardown — order is safe.) The HTTP server stays up so the
     already-rendered
     card and the did this match? feedback endpoint keep working.
     - /api/status response gains ended: <bool> (the existing ended flag,
     line 260).
     - New POST /api/shutdown: idempotent — teardownContainer(), respond {ok},
     then
     process.exit(0) on next tick.
     - New POST /api/abandon (D1): sets ended = true, emits a session_abandoned
     chrome
     event into the trace (kept on disk for CLI rejudge), does not judge, does
     not
     write an assessment, then teardown + respond + exit like shutdown. The
     queue item
     stays ready automatically: reconcile derives done only from an assessment
     file,
     and .used already points at this session id — reconcile keeps status ready
     because no assessment exists (verify in test).
     - SessionConfig gains targetId?: string and appUrl?: string.

     server/src/cli.ts (session cmd, :343): derive targetId from problemDir
     matching targets/<id>/problems/<item>; pass targetId + appUrl (from new
     IP_APP_URL env) into runSession.

     server/src/app.ts:
     - sessionLive() → parse the JSON body; live = 200 && !ended.
     - /api/launch: before the existing live-409, if :3200 answers but reports
     ended: true, POST its /api/shutdown, poll the port free (up to ~5s), then
     proceed. A genuinely live session still 409s. Pass IP_APP_URL: 
     http://localhost:${cfg.port} into spawnDetached.
     - New POST /api/session-kill: proxy to :3200 /api/abandon; 502 if
     unreachable.

     server/src/chrome.ts: header gains <a href="${appUrl}/#/t/${targetId}">← 
     back to plan</a> (fallback appUrl root when no targetId). This is the
     session page's
     first link (ISSUE-004).

     server/src/client/session.js:
     - Store interval handles (clock :24, pollStatus :44, pollMessages :90);
     clear all
     three in endSession() and in render(card) (ISSUE-012).
     - On card render: hide the (now-dead — container is gone) iframe and expand
     the card
     to the full area, with a prominent ← back to your plan link using the same
     header href (ISSUE-004).

     server/src/client/app.js + appPage() masthead: when session_live, next to
     the existing "session live" link add an end session control → confirm() →
     POST /api/session-kill → refresh(true). Copy makes discard semantics
     explicit:
     "End without grading? The attempt is discarded (recoverable via rejudge)."

     ISSUE-013 disappears with the above (nobody needs lsof anymore).

     B. Timeline truth (ISSUE-002, 010, 011 — D2/D3/D4)

     server/src/queue.ts bucketIntoDays:
     - Emit done-today items as a new row kind {kind: 'done-today', items}
     immediately
     before the TODAY row (D2). Done items keep their existing past-band
     behavior for
     earlier dates.
     - TODAY row semantics: if a next item exists, unchanged; if none remains
     today but
     the season has pending items, TODAY carries note: 'done for today' + the
     next
     item's date; if zero non-done/skipped items remain, emit
     {kind: 'complete'} instead of an empty TODAY (season-complete state).
     - Collapse runs of ≥2 consecutive empty future day rows into
     {kind: 'quiet', count, from, to} (D4). Past band and item-bearing days keep
     their date anchors. The existing collapsed far-stretch row stays.

     server/src/client/app.js renderSeason:
     - done-today rows: past-style ✓ rows pinned above TODAY.
     - complete row: "season complete — N rounds done" + link to the plan's
     feedback
     (and the existing past-interview branch remains for after the date).
     - quiet rows: one thin centered "· N quiet days ·" row.
     - Future pending rows gain a small Generate button; a future ready row
     gains
     Start (D3). Both reuse the existing wireTimeline classes — the server's
     one-generating-at-a-time 409 and session-live 409 already guard them.

     C. Generation truth (ISSUE-003, 007, 008)

     server/src/app.ts spawnGeneration: before spawn, mkdir the dir and write
     <dir>/.generating as JSON {pid: child.pid, started_at: ISO} right after
     spawn;
     remove it in the close handler. /api/retry and /api/rebuild refuse (409)
     while a
     live .generating exists (prevents the double-agent race).

     sweepOrphanedGenerations: if .generating exists, read the pid and probe
     with
     process.kill(pid, 0) — alive → leave the item generating (not our child
     anymore;
     terminal markers will flip it via reconcile); dead → write .failed as
     today. Delete
     stale .generating whenever .validated/.failed exists.

     /api/state: items with status generating gain
     generating: {since, files, phase} — since from the marker, files = non-dot
     file
     count in the dir, phase = 'finalizing' when problem.json exists else
     'building'.

     server/src/client/app.js: the generating row shows a live elapsed clock
     ("building — 3m 20s · 9 files · usually 5–8 min"), phase text, and the bar
     becomes
     determinate: width = min(95%, elapsed / 8min) (8 min = the real timeout
     wall,

      176 +/** Buffer a small GET from the IDE (used only for the workben
          +ch boot HTML,                                                 
      177 + *  which is a few hundred KB). Rejects on non-200 so the call
          +er falls back                                                 
      178 + *  to the transparent proxy. */                              
      179 +function fetchIdeHtml(port: number, urlPath: string): Promise<
          +string> {                                                     
      180 +  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {                   
      181 +    const r = http.get({ host: '127.0.0.1', port, path: urlPat
          +h, timeout: 5_000 }, (res) => {                               
      182 +      if (res.statusCode !== 200) {                           
      183 +        res.resume();                                         
      184 +        return reject(new Error(`ide ${res.statusCode}`));    
      185 +      }                                                       
      186 +      let body = '';                                          
      187 +      res.on('data', (d) => (body += d));                     
      188 +      res.on('end', () => resolve(body));                     
      189 +    });                                                       
      190 +    r.on('error', reject);                                    
      191 +    r.on('timeout', () => {                                   
      192 +      r.destroy();                                            
      193 +      reject(new Error('ide timeout'));                       
      194 +    });                                                       
      195 +  });                                                         
      196 +}

⏺ Read the whole path. Here's my assessment — the short version is that you've 
  already built the machinery to relax this, and it's sitting unused.

  The restriction is tighter than the architecture requires

  The prompt forbids narrowing the search space "in any way — even mildly." But
  look at what nudge: true does downstream. It isn't decorative bookkeeping:

  - timeline.ts:105 annotates the judge's view: [NUDGE — this narrowed the 
  search; what follows was prompted, not self-directed]
  - judge-session.md:58: prompted behavior "can still be assessed (did they use
  the nudge well?) but must not be credited as self-directed initiative"
  - gap-graph.ts:148: a gap that fires only after a nudge marks the session
  uninformative — it neither advances nor resets a remediation streak

  So the system was designed expecting the interviewer to sometimes narrow the
  search, and to account for it honestly. Then the prompt said "never do that."
  The contamination problem you'd worry about when adding help is already 
  solved. That's real headroom you're not using.

  The actual gap isn't restrictiveness — it's that "stuck" doesn't exist

  Unprompted turns fire on a clock: every 4 minutes, gated only on
  hasFailingRun. Nothing looks at whether you're progressing. Which produces the
  wrong behavior at both ends:

  - Cruising candidate → interrupted every 4 minutes anyway (noise)
  - Candidate stuck 20 minutes → same 4-minute clock, and the only lever the
  prompt permits is more pressure

  That's the asymmetry worth fixing. Pressure scales the wrong way. Applied to
  someone genuinely stuck, "you have 12 minutes, what's your leading theory?"
  produces flailing, not learning. The interviewer's single tool gets actively
  worse exactly when the candidate needs it most.

  Why real interviewers hint — and why it argues for your product, not against 
  it

  Good interviewers escalate hints when a candidate is truly stuck, for a reason
  that isn't kindness: a stuck candidate produces no signal. Forty minutes of
  silent flailing tells the interviewer nothing about implement, verify, or
  reflect.

  Your own QA proved this on your own product. My 18-second session returned 5 
  of 6 dimensions unassessable. A 40-minute stuck session returns nearly the
  same card — and that's a wasted rep during a season where you have maybe
  fifteen.

  So "harder than the real round" is right on one axis and questionable on
  another:

  ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │            Axis            │       Should it be harder than real?       │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Does it hand you the       │ Yes, stay hard. Never confirm a theory,    │
  │ answer?                    │ never name the location.                   │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Does it let the session    │ Probably not. A no-signal session costs    │
  │ produce nothing?           │ you a rep and a judge card.                │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The distinction that dissolves your tension: a hint that unblocks is not a 
  hint that solves. The current prompt collapses them because it bans narrowing
  "in any way."

  What I'd actually consider

  Detect stuck from the trace, not the clock. Everything needed is already
  emitted: time since last edit, the same test failure repeating, file opens
  with no edits, silence. Stuck ≈ N minutes with no new information entering the
  session.

  A hint ladder where every rung above 0 is nudge: true:
  - 0 (today): probe. "What have you ruled out?"
  - 1: method, not location. "What would you need to see to tell those two
  theories apart?" — teaches instrumentation, narrows nothing.
  - 2: scope. "The failing test crosses three subsystems. Which haven't you
  looked at?" — narrows, marked.
  - 3 (late round only): direct pointer, so the round yields some
  implement/verify signal.

  Rungs 1–3 already have honest accounting waiting for them.

  And the more product-shaped idea: hardness is currently a global prompt
  constant, but it looks like it wants to be a round property — the same
  "capabilities, not categories" move you already made. Week 1 of a season wants
  a teaching interviewer; the week before Palantir wants none. Right now every
  rep in a 12-round season is equally brutal, which is probably wrong at both
  ends.

  The blocker before any of this

  Nothing evaluates interviewer behavior. The 30 tests in interviewer.test.ts
  are all guard/parse/render units — leak catching, JSON tolerance. The gauntlet
  scores the judge, and touches the interviewer only for intent-check
  classification. So today you could change that prompt and have no way to tell
  whether it got better.

  If you want to tune this, I'd build the interviewer eval first — persona
  traces of a stuck candidate and a cruising one, scored on "did it leak," "did
  it help at the right moment," "did it mark the nudge." That's the same shape
  as the existing gauntlet and it makes every future tuning decision measurable
  instead of vibes.

  Want me to scope that as a proper plan, or dig further into any one of these
  first?

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I need to correct something I told you an hour ago. Let me lay out the design
  space properly.

  Correction first: nudges cost you less than I implied

  I said help trades against measurement 1:1. That was true in the label era,
  not now. Today (gap-graph.ts:194) a dimension is uninformative only when
  verdict === 'unassessable' or a weak verdict whose citations were all
  stripped. Nudges don't contaminate the gap graph at all anymore.

  The only place nudge survives is the judge's timeline annotation ([NUDGE — 
  what follows was prompted, not self-directed]), where it's soft guidance to
  one prompt.

  Two consequences, and the second is the interesting one:

  1. Adding help is cheaper than I said — no automatic memory-layer penalty.
  2. It's arguably too cheap. A heavily-hinted session currently writes to your
  profile as if it were self-directed, because the only thing standing between a
  nudge and your permanent record is the judge choosing to discount it in
  prose. That's the real gap in the accounting, and it gets worse the moment you
  escalate more.

  So escalation and accounting have to move together. Now the design space.

  ---
  The four decisions that actually matter

  1. Who picks the escalation level — code or model?

  Your codebase answers this everywhere else: code selects the closed thing, the
  model fills the open thing. CHECK_REQUIREMENT_BLOCKS picks the proof, the
  model writes the problem. Tags are deriveMemoryTags, never model-authored.
  parseTurn defaults nudge to true when absent because you already don't trust
  the model's self-report.

  Applied here: code computes a ceiling from the trace, the model writes the 
  sentence under it. The prompt gets "you may escalate to at most rung 2," never
  "decide if they're stuck." That keeps "how much help did this session get" a
  number rather than a vibe — which is exactly what the accounting gap above
  needs.

  The alternative (model judges stuckness) fails a specific way: a model asked
  "are they stuck?" wants to help. It'll say yes.

  2. Stuck isn't one state — and the right hint differs by kind

  The trace can already distinguish at least three, and they want opposite
  responses:

  ┌───────────┬────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Kind    │      Trace signature       │           What helps           │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Blind     │ many file opens, few edits │ method: "what would tell those │
  │ search    │                            │  apart?"                       │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │           │ repeated edits to one      │ challenge: "what makes you     │
  │ Anchored  │ file, same test still      │ confident the read path is     │
  │           │ failing                    │ fine?"                         │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Frozen    │ no edits, no opens, no     │ re-engage: "where are you      │
  │           │ speech                     │ right now?"                    │
  └───────────┴────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  Anchoring is already the disposition you flagged as most valuable in TODOS #7
  ("anchors on a preconceived cause instead of instrumenting"). Detecting it
  live is the same computation.

  3. The ladder — and where it should stop

  0  probe        "What have you ruled out?"                    nudge: false
  1  method       "What would you need to see to tell           nudge: false   ←
  free
                   those two theories apart?"
  2  reframe      "You've been in the write path a while —      nudge: true
                   what makes you confident the read side
                   is correct?"
  3  scope        "The failing test crosses three modules.      nudge: true
                   Two you haven't opened."
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  4  pointer      "Look at how the ledger applies the           ← I'd cut this
                   snapshot."

  Rung 1 is the find here. It narrows nothing, so nudge: false — genuinely free
  help, and it teaches instrumentation, which is two of your six dimensions.
  Most of the value you're looking for might live entirely at rung 1 without
  touching the hard rule.

  I'd cut rung 4, because you already solved it better: session.js has "show me 
  the bug (spoils a re-run)" on the card, gated on solved, chosen by the
  candidate after the round. Terminal stuckness already has an escape hatch that
  doesn't spoil the session or break the hard rule. The interviewer never needs
  to point.

  4. The trigger should make early harder than it is today

  This is the part that dissolves your tension. Right now pressure fires on a
  4-minute clock from the first failing test — so a cruising candidate gets
  nagged at minute 4, and a stuck one gets identical treatment at minute 35.

  If the ceiling is a function of stuck-duration × time-remaining, it
  redistributes rather than softens:

  - Minute 8, stuck 5 min → rung 0 only. Stricter than today (no clock nag).
  Early struggle is the exercise.
  - Minute 30, stuck 12 min → rung 2 available.
  - Minute 40, stuck 15 min → rung 3, because a round that ends with nothing
  observed is a wasted rep.

  Net: harder than the real round for the first half, more helpful than today in
  the last quarter. You get both things you wanted.

  ---
  Two things I'd want your read on

  The accounting question is now the sharp one. Since nudges no longer
  contaminate anything mechanically, do you want escalation to write something
  durable — e.g. the session records its max rung, and the gap graph treats a
  rung-3 session as uninformative for the dimensions that hint touched? That
  restores the honesty guarantee you originally built, at the cost of making
  heavy-help sessions teach the memory layer less. I lean yes, but it's a real
  trade and it's yours.

  And: should this vary across a season? Hardness is a global prompt constant
  today. It looks like it wants to be a round property — week 1 of a season
  teaching, the week before Palantir giving nothing. That's the same
  "capabilities, not categories" move, but it's scope growth, and there's a case
  for one honest setting instead.

  One thing worth flagging regardless of what we pick: stuck detection is 
  valuable even where hints are forbidden. On an OA round (interviewer: false)
  nobody should speak — but "you spent 22 minutes without opening a new file" is
  a genuine observation the judge could use on the card. That decouples
  detection from hinting, and makes the detector earn its keep on every round
  shape.

  Which of these do you want to push on before I turn it into a plan?

✻ Crunched for 2m 2s

Waiting on your read on two questions: whether heavy hinting should reduce what a session teaches the memory layer, and whether hardness should vary across a season.

⏺ Voice is off because there's no ElevenLabs API key in the environment. It's
  not a bug and nothing is broken.

  session.ts:364-365:
  const elevenKey = process.env.ELEVENLABS_API_KEY ??
  process.env.IP_ELEVENLABS_KEY ?? '';
  const voiceEnabled = (cfg.voice ?? true) && elevenKey.length > 0;

  Voice is default-on (cfg.voice ?? true) — it just requires that key, and
  neither variable is set in your shell. There's also no .env loading anywhere
  in the repo (no dotenv), so the key has to be exported in the shell that
  starts the app.

  Your live interviewer is fine. Voice is only the audio layer — TTS for its
  turns, STT for your speech. The interviewer still reads your trace, answers
  spec questions, applies pressure, and replies in the chat panel. You're
  currently getting a text-only interviewer.

  To turn it on

  The key has to be in the app server's environment, because /api/launch spawns
  sessions with {...process.env} — so exporting it in a new terminal won't reach
  a session launched by an app server started earlier. Run this yourself (I
  won't handle the key):

  ! export ELEVENLABS_API_KEY='your-key' && kill $(lsof -nP -iTCP:3300
  -sTCP:LISTEN -t) && npx tsx server/src/cli.ts app

  Then start a fresh session — the header should read voice: on and the mute
  button should appear. Your current session won't pick it up; it captured its
  env at spawn.

  Worth knowing before you do: voice.ts enforces a hard per-session USD cap with
  published ElevenLabs rates, and at 100% it degrades to text rather than
  silently muting.

  Two real findings from this

  "voice: off" is a state with no cause and no remedy — exactly what you hit
  ("idk how to turn it on"). Same class as the "app server unreachable" message
  I fixed earlier today, which reported a client crash as a dead server. It
  should distinguish no key configured (actionable: here's the variable) from
  disabled for this round from budget exhausted. Right now all three render
  identically.

  The key isn't documented anywhere — not in README, CLAUDE.md, or docs. Nothing
  tells you it exists or that voice silently degrades without it. For a feature
  you built the whole ambient-sensor architecture around, that's a rough

⏺ Done, committed, 290 tests green.

  Answering your question directly

  No, it wasn't auto-loading — and the key genuinely isn't set anywhere. I
  checked four places: no .env in the repo, not in any shell profile, not
  visible to a login shell, and not in the running app server's environment.
  Whatever terminal you set it in didn't reach the app server.

  That's the trap, and it's worth internalizing: /api/launch spawns sessions
  with {...process.env}, so a session inherits the app server's environment, not
  your current shell. Exporting the key in a new tab does nothing for an app
  server started in an older one.

  Now it does auto-load

  cli.ts reads a repo-root .env via native process.loadEnvFile — Node 20.19.5
  has it, so zero new dependencies, which suits a repo with almost none. Every
  entry point routes through cli.ts, and the app hands its environment to what
  it spawns, so one load covers app, sessions, and generators.

  Verified precedence: a shell export still wins. loadEnvFile never overwrites
  an already-set variable, so this can't silently override you.

  To use it:
  cp .env.example .env      # then put your key in it, and restart the app
  server

  .env.example documents ELEVENLABS_API_KEY, ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, and the IP_*
  per-session overrides. It needed a !.env.example exception since .gitignore
  had a blanket .env.* — otherwise the documentation would have shipped to
  nobody.

  The indicator

  /api/status now carries a reason, and the chip renders it:

  - voice: off (no API key) — tooltip names the variable and the fix
  - voice: off (disabled for this round) — IP_VOICE=0

  Plus the same at server startup in the log. All three say the interviewer 
  still runs, text-only — because that's the part you couldn't tell, and it's
  what made a missing credential look like a broken feature.

  One note on your current session: it captured its environment at spawn, so it
  won't pick any of this up. Restart the app server after adding the key, then
  launch fresh.

  I did not touch the key itself — set that yourself in .env.

  Ready to get back to scoping interviewer escalation whenever you are. The open
  questions from before still stand: whether escalation should write something
  durable to the record now that nudges no longer contaminate the gap graph, and
  whether hardness becomes a round property or stays one honest global setting.

⏺ No, you never made one here. The only .env* file in interview_prep is the
  .env.example I created twenty minutes ago. There has never been a real .env in
  this repo.

  What almost certainly happened: you have ELEVENLABS_API_KEY in roughly a dozen
  other projects — ReLU, VibeLearn, algo_learning_tool, jezy, shu-xiang,
  ai_caller_225, private-chronicles, and more. So the muscle memory of "the key
  is in env" is real, just not for this repo.

  Convenient detail: they all use the exact variable name Zenkai expects,
  ELEVENLABS_API_KEY (alongside an ELEVENLABS_VOICE_ID this codebase doesn't
  use).

  To fix it

  Run this yourself so the key never passes through me:

  ! cp ~/Documents/GitHub/ReLU/.env .env && grep -v ELEVENLABS_VOICE_ID .env >
  .env.tmp && mv .env.tmp .env

  Then trim it to just the keys Zenkai reads (ELEVENLABS_API_KEY, optionally
  ANTHROPIC_API_KEY) — ReLU's file probably carries unrelated credentials you
  don't want sitting in this repo. Or just open .env.example, copy it to .env,
  and paste the one key in.

  After that, restart the app server and launch a fresh session:

  ! kill $(lsof -nP -iTCP:3300 -sTCP:LISTEN -t) && npx tsx server/src/cli.ts app

  The header should read voice: on with a mute button. If it still says voice: 
  off (no API key), the .env didn't land in the repo root — that's the only
  place cli.ts looks.

  One thing worth knowing given the spread: you have that same ElevenLabs key in
  ~10 local .env files. Rotating it later means finding all of them. Not
  urgent, just a rake in the yard.
